Um… I came in because my brain feels like it’s kind of stalling out. Like at work, I’ll have a test report open, or even just a simple CAD note, and I’ll sit there for an hour staring at it and I can’t decide what the next sentence should be, or even which checkbox to click.

It’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the work is new to me. I mean, I used to be pretty good at keeping things moving—well, except when the software freezes, which happens all the time and everyone jokes about it—but this feels different. It’s like I’m the one freezing.

Yeah… guilty, I guess. Maybe more than I should be.

It’s mostly stupid little things from the lab, like if I mislabeled something for a minute or had to redo a setup, or if I think maybe I slowed someone down. I used to be really into printing little miniatures, like painting them and getting the supports just right—well, except when the resin would fail and make this weird pancake thing on the build plate—but I’ve had the same half-finished figure sitting there for months.\n\nAnd even stuff that’s supposed to help, like yoga or walking in the woods, it just feels like another task on a list. Like I should do it, but I don’t actually… want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I used to care about getting the tiny details right, like supports and sanding and painting, and now the same half-finished figure has just been sitting there for months.\n\nAnd yoga, or going for walks in the woods—those used to feel good, or at least like something I wanted to do. It’s not like I’m trying to be slow, but in meetings people have started kind of jumping in and finishing my sentences because I’m taking too long to answer. Which is embarrassing, because I do know what I’m trying to say, it’s just like it has to come through molasses first.\n\nAnd even just walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly heavy, like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. I keep thinking about the floor tiles there, actually, because one of them has this little chipped corner and I notice it every time now… but, yeah, I guess I do feel slower than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess. Yeah… sometimes. Not like I have some big plan written out or anything, but… on my commute, I’ll catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t arrive anywhere.

I’ll sit there with the hazard lights on, and then I start worrying someone’s going to think my car broke down, or that I’m blocking something, and it becomes this whole… anyway.

So, yes. I’ve had thoughts. I don’t like saying that out loud.

Sorry, that’s a weird comparison—I’ve been dealing with these awful screens at work, and one of them has this flicker that nobody else seems to notice.\n\nBut, yeah… down, I guess. I’m not sure how to measure it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. On edge like nervous? I don’t think of it that way exactly. I mean, I get tense when people are waiting on me, but that’s also kind of normal at work, right? There’s always some deadline or someone needing a drawing revised, and then the printer jams even though it’s supposedly “fixed”—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed being anxious, specifically. It’s more that I don’t know what I’m feeling half the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to be really into printing little miniatures, like painting them and getting the supports just right—well, except when the resin would fail and make this weird pancake thing on the build plate—but I’ve had the same half-finished figure sitting there for months.\n\nAnd even stuff that’s supposed to help, like yoga or walking in the woods, it just feels like another task on a list. Like I should do it, but I don’t actually… want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I kind of already said it, but the miniature printer thing is probably the clearest example. I used to care about getting the tiny details right, like supports and sanding and painting, and now the same half-finished figure has just been sitting there for months.\n\nAnd yoga, or going for walks in the woods—those used to feel good, or at least like something I wanted to do. Now it’s more like, “Okay, I should do that,” like checking off a chore. It doesn’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been tracking it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not pacing exactly. I’m not, like, wearing a path into the carpet or anything.

It’s more that I’m… slow. People in meetings have started finishing my sentences because I take too long to get the answer out, which is frustrating because I know what I mean, it just feels like it has to travel through mud first. And even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels like I’m moving through thick syrup. I don’t know why I said syrup—there’s this one hallway at work where the floor is always kind of sticky near the vending machines—but, yeah. Not constant motion. More the opposite.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I came in because my brain feels like it’s kind of stalling out. Like at work, I’ll have a test report open, or even just a simple CAD note, and I’ll sit there for an hour staring at it and I can’t decide what the next sentence should be, or even which checkbox to click.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the work is new to me. I mean, I used to be pretty good at keeping things moving—well, except when the software freezes, which happens all the time and everyone jokes about it—but this feels different. It’s like I’m the one freezing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sad is the right word. I mean, maybe? It’s more like everything feels kind of muted, like when a monitor is on but the brightness is turned way down. Sorry, that’s a weird comparison—I’ve been dealing with these awful screens at work, and one of them has this flicker that nobody else seems to notice.\n\nBut, yeah… down, I guess. I’m not sure how to measure it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. On edge like nervous? I don’t think of it that way exactly. I mean, I get tense when people are waiting on me, but that’s also kind of normal at work, right? There’s always some deadline or someone needing a drawing revised, and then the printer jams even though it’s supposedly “fixed”—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed being anxious, specifically. It’s more that I don’t know what I’m feeling half the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to be really into printing little miniatures, like painting them and getting the supports just right—well, except when the resin would fail and make this weird pancake thing on the build plate—but I’ve had the same half-finished figure sitting there for months.\n\nAnd even stuff that’s supposed to help, like yoga or walking in the woods, it just feels like another task on a list. Like I should do it, but I don’t actually… want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I kind of already said it, but the miniature printer thing is probably the clearest example. I used to care about getting the tiny details right, like supports and sanding and painting, and now the same half-finished figure has just been sitting there for months.\n\nAnd yoga, or going for walks in the woods—those used to feel good, or at least like something I wanted to do. Now it’s more like, “Okay, I should do that,” like checking off a chore. It doesn’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been tracking it. Appetite, I mean, some days I just eat because it’s time, not because I’m hungry, but I don’t know if that’s different enough to count.\n\nSleep is… hard to say too. I’ll go to bed, and then I start thinking about whether I set up the print files correctly at work, or if I forgot to answer an email, and then somehow it’s like I’m reorganizing my whole desk in my head. But I don’t know if that’s a sleep pattern change exactly. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself much—my scale is kind of shoved under the bathroom shelf with, like, extra shampoo bottles and stuff. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I guess? But I also wear the same loose hoodie most of the time, so that’s probably not a great measure.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a significant change, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving or reacting more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m trying to be slow, but in meetings people have started kind of jumping in and finishing my sentences because I’m taking too long to answer. Which is embarrassing, because I do know what I’m trying to say, it’s just like it has to come through molasses first.\n\nAnd even just walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly heavy, like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. I keep thinking about the floor tiles there, actually, because one of them has this little chipped corner and I notice it every time now… but, yeah, I guess I do feel slower than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess. Maybe more than I should be.\n\nIt’s mostly stupid little things from the lab, like if I mislabeled something for a minute or had to redo a setup, or if I think maybe I slowed someone down. I’ll replay it over and over, like while I’m brushing my teeth or trying to make coffee, and then suddenly it’s not just, “I made a mistake,” it’s… “I’m bad at this, I shouldn’t be trusted,” and then it turns into “maybe I’m just a bad person.”\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic. It’s not like one specific huge thing. It’s tiny things. But my brain keeps making them feel like proof that I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still, or is it more that you're feeling slowed down physically?\n\nPatient: More slowed down, I think. I’m not really fidgety… I mean, I pick at my sleeve sometimes, but that’s more because there’s this loose thread on it and it catches on things, and then I notice it for like ten minutes.\n\nBut, yeah, mostly slowed. Like in meetings, people have started finishing my sentences because I take too long to answer. I mean, I used to be pretty good at keeping things moving—well, except when the software freezes, which happens all the time and everyone jokes about it—but this feels different. It’s like I’m the one freezing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sad is the right word. I mean, maybe? Sorry, that’s a weird comparison—I’ve been dealing with these awful screens at work, and one of them has this flicker that nobody else seems to notice.\n\nBut, yeah… down, I guess. I’m not sure how to measure it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. On edge like nervous? I don’t think of it that way exactly. I mean, I get tense when people are waiting on me, but that’s also kind of normal at work, right? It’s more that I don’t know what I’m feeling half the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to be really into printing little miniatures, like painting them and getting the supports just right—well, except when the resin would fail and make this weird pancake thing on the build plate—but I’ve had the same half-finished figure sitting there for months.\n\nAnd even stuff that’s supposed to help, like yoga or walking in the woods, it just feels like another task on a list. Like I should do it, but I don’t actually… want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I kind of already said it, but the miniature printer thing is probably the clearest example. I used to care about getting the tiny details right, like supports and sanding and painting, and now the same half-finished figure has just been sitting there for months.\n\nAnd yoga, or going for walks in the woods—those used to feel good, or at least like something I wanted to do. Now it’s more like, “Okay, I should do that,” like checking off a chore. It doesn’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been tracking it. Appetite, I mean, some days I just eat because it’s time, not because I’m hungry, but I don’t know if that’s different enough to count.\n\nSleep is… hard to say too. I’ll go to bed, and then I start thinking about whether I set up the print files correctly at work, or if I forgot to answer an email, and then somehow it’s like I’m reorganizing my whole desk in my head. But I don’t know if that’s a sleep pattern change exactly. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself much—my scale is kind of shoved under the bathroom shelf with, like, extra shampoo bottles and stuff. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I guess? But I also wear the same loose hoodie most of the time, so that’s probably not a great measure.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a significant change, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving or reacting more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m trying to be slow, but in meetings people have started kind of jumping in and finishing my sentences because I’m taking too long to answer. It’s not like one specific huge thing. It’s tiny things. But my brain keeps making them feel like proof that I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still, or is it more that you're feeling slowed down physically?\n\nPatient: More slowed down, I think. I’m not really fidgety… I mean, I pick at my sleeve sometimes, but that’s more because there’s this loose thread on it and it catches on things, and then I notice it for like ten minutes.\n\nBut, yeah, mostly slowed. I used to joke that the hallway lights make everything feel like a hospital basement, but I don’t think it’s the lights. It’s just me being slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: No, not pacing exactly. Sorry, that’s a weird comparison—I’ve been dealing with these awful screens at work, and one of them has this flicker that nobody else seems to notice.\n\nBut, yeah… down, I guess. I’m not sure how to measure it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. On edge like nervous? I don’t think of it that way exactly. I mean, I get tense when people are waiting on me, but that’s also kind of normal at work, right? There’s always some deadline or someone needing a drawing revised, and then the printer jams even though it’s supposedly “fixed”—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed being anxious, specifically. It’s more that I don’t know what I’m feeling half the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to be really into printing little miniatures, like painting them and getting the supports just right—well, except when the resin would fail and make this weird pancake thing on the build plate—but I’ve had the same half-finished figure sitting there for months.\n\nAnd even stuff that’s supposed to help, like yoga or walking in the woods, it just feels like another task on a list. Like I should do it, but I don’t actually… want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I kind of already said it, but the miniature printer thing is probably the clearest example. I used to care about getting the tiny details right, like supports and sanding and painting, and now the same half-finished figure has just been sitting there for months.\n\nAnd yoga, or going for walks in the woods—those used to feel good, or at least like something I wanted to do. Now it’s more like, “Okay, I should do that,” like checking off a chore. It doesn’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been tracking it. Appetite, I mean, some days I just eat because it’s time, not because I’m hungry, but I don’t know if that’s different enough to count.\n\nSleep is… hard to say too. I’ll go to bed, and then I start thinking about whether I set up the print files correctly at work, or if I forgot to answer an email, and then somehow it’s like I’m reorganizing my whole desk in my head. But I don’t know if that’s a sleep pattern change exactly. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself much—my scale is kind of shoved under the bathroom shelf with, like, extra shampoo bottles and stuff. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I guess? But I also wear the same loose hoodie most of the time, so that’s probably not a great measure.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a significant change, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving or reacting more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m trying to be slow, but in meetings people have started kind of jumping in and finishing my sentences because I’m taking too long to answer. Which is embarrassing, because I do know what I’m trying to say, it’s just like it has to come through molasses first.\n\nAnd even just walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly heavy, like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. I keep thinking about the floor tiles there, actually, because one of them has this little chipped corner and I notice it every time now… but, yeah, I guess I do feel slower than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess. Maybe more than I should be.\n\nIt’s mostly stupid little things from the lab, like if I mislabeled something for a minute or had to redo a setup, or if I think maybe I slowed someone down. I’ll replay it over and over, like while I’m brushing my teeth or trying to make coffee, and then suddenly it’s not just, “I made a mistake,” it’s… “I’m bad at this, I shouldn’t be trusted,” and then it turns into “maybe I’m just a bad person.”\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic. It’s not like one specific huge thing. It’s tiny things. But my brain keeps making them feel like proof that I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still, or is it more that you're feeling slowed down physically?\n\nPatient: More slowed down, I think. I’m not really fidgety… I mean, I pick at my sleeve sometimes, but that’s more because there’s this loose thread on it and it catches on things, and then I notice it for like ten minutes.\n\nBut, yeah, mostly slowed. Like in meetings, people have started finishing my sentences because I take too long to answer. And walking from my desk to the test chamber feels… heavy, like I’m moving through thick syrup.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think so? I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nMy scale is under this basket of laundry in the bathroom, and I keep meaning to move it, but then I notice the basket has all these mismatched socks in it and I start sorting them, and then I’m late for work, so… I don’t actually know. My clothes don’t feel hugely different, I guess. So, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I have some big plan written out or anything, but… on my commute, I’ll catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t arrive anywhere.\n\nAnd then sometimes, when I’m driving, there’s this thought about steering off the road, and it scares me. Like, actually scares me enough that I grip the wheel really hard and pull over for a bit. I’ll sit there with the hazard lights on, and then I start worrying someone’s going to think my car broke down, or that I’m blocking something, and it becomes this whole… anyway.\n\nSo, yes. I’ve had thoughts. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself restless or having an urge to move around when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Not really an urge to move, no. It’s just me being slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: No, not pacing exactly. I don’t know why I said syrup—there’s this one hallway at work where the floor is always kind of sticky near the vending machines—but, yeah. Not constant motion. More the opposite.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an inner restlessness that makes you want to move around?\n\nPatient: No… not inner restlessness, exactly. I don’t feel like I need to get up and move around.\n\nIt’s more like everything takes me too long. People at work have started finishing my sentences in meetings because I’m just… slow getting the answer out. Like my body and my words are lagging behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depression is interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think it is.\n\nWork has been the clearest, I guess. I missed a pretty important project deadline, which… I don’t usually do. Or I didn’t used to. And now people are having to double-check my reports, or take over pieces of them, and it’s hard because I used to be the one who could just handle that stuff.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s not like I’m doing much better. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I came in because my brain feels like it’s kind of stalling out. Like at work, I’ll have a test report open, or even just a simple CAD note, and I’ll sit there for an hour staring at it and I can’t decide what the next sentence should be, or even which checkbox to click.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because it’s not like the work is new to me. I mean, I used to be pretty good at keeping things moving—well, except when the software freezes, which happens all the time and everyone jokes about it—but this feels different. I’m not sure how to measure it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. On edge like nervous? I don’t think of it that way exactly. I mean, I get tense when people are waiting on me, but that’s also kind of normal at work, right? It’s more that I don’t know what I’m feeling half the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Now it’s more like, “Okay, I should do that,” like checking off a chore. I’ll go to bed, and then I start thinking about whether I set up the print files correctly at work, or if I forgot to answer an email, and then somehow it’s like I’m reorganizing my whole desk in my head. But I don’t know if that’s a sleep pattern change exactly. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself much—my scale is kind of shoved under the bathroom shelf with, like, extra shampoo bottles and stuff. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I guess? But I also wear the same loose hoodie most of the time, so that’s probably not a great measure.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a significant change, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving or reacting more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m trying to be slow, but in meetings people have started kind of jumping in and finishing my sentences because I’m taking too long to answer. Which is embarrassing, because I do know what I’m trying to say, it’s just like it has to come through molasses first.\n\nAnd even just walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly heavy, like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. I keep thinking about the floor tiles there, actually, because one of them has this little chipped corner and I notice it every time now… but, yeah, I guess I do feel slower than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess. Maybe more than I should be.\n\nIt’s mostly stupid little things from the lab, like if I mislabeled something for a minute or had to redo a setup, or if I think maybe I slowed someone down. I’ll replay it over and over, like while I’m brushing my teeth or trying to make coffee, and then suddenly it’s not just, “I made a mistake,” it’s… “I’m bad at this, I shouldn’t be trusted,” and then it turns into “maybe I’m just a bad person.”\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic. It’s not like one specific huge thing. It’s tiny things. But my brain keeps making them feel like proof that I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still, or is it more that you're feeling slowed down physically?\n\nPatient: More slowed down, I think. I’m not really fidgety… I mean, I pick at my sleeve sometimes, but that’s more because there’s this loose thread on it and it catches on things, and then I notice it for like ten minutes.\n\nBut, yeah, mostly slowed. Like in meetings, people have started finishing my sentences because I take too long to answer. And walking from my desk to the test chamber feels… heavy, like I’m moving through thick syrup.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think so? I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nMy scale is under this basket of laundry in the bathroom, and I keep meaning to move it, but then I notice the basket has all these mismatched socks in it and I start sorting them, and then I’m late for work, so… I don’t actually know. My clothes don’t feel hugely different, I guess. So, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I have some big plan written out or anything, but… on my commute, I’ll catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t arrive anywhere.\n\nAnd then sometimes, when I’m driving, there’s this thought about steering off the road, and it scares me. Like, actually scares me enough that I grip the wheel really hard and pull over for a bit. I’ll sit there with the hazard lights on, and then I start worrying someone’s going to think my car broke down, or that I’m blocking something, and it becomes this whole… anyway.\n\nSo, yes. I’ve had thoughts. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself restless or having an urge to move around when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Not really an urge to move, no. It’s more the opposite, like I get stuck in slow motion.\n\nIn meetings, people have started finishing my sentences because I take so long to answer, which is… embarrassing. And even just walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly hard, like I’m moving through thick syrup. I used to joke that the hallway lights make everything feel like a hospital basement, but I don’t think it’s the lights. It’s just me being slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself pacing back and forth or feeling like you need to be in constant motion?\n\nPatient: No, not pacing exactly. I’m not, like, wearing a path into the carpet or anything.\n\nIt’s more that I’m… slow. People in meetings have started finishing my sentences because I take too long to get the answer out, which is frustrating because I know what I mean, it just feels like it has to travel through mud first. More the opposite.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an inner restlessness that makes you want to move around?\n\nPatient: No… not inner restlessness, exactly. I don’t feel like I need to get up and move around.\n\nIt’s more like everything takes me too long. Like my body and my words are lagging behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depression is interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, I think it is.\n\nWork has been the clearest, I guess. I missed a pretty important project deadline, which… I don’t usually do. Or I didn’t used to. And now people are having to double-check my reports, or take over pieces of them, and it’s hard because I used to be the one who could just handle that stuff.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s not like I’m doing much better. I’ve mostly stopped seeing people. Even the little routines that kept me kind of steady, I’m not really doing them anymore. I keep thinking I’ll get back to it, like if I just reorganize my apartment or make a better list— I used to have this notebook with color-coded tabs, which sounds very engineering-tech of me, I know—but I don’t really follow through. So… yeah. It’s interfering. A lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… most of the time, I guess I don’t really feel pleasure from things anymore. Or not the way I used to.

Like, my miniature printer has had the same half-finished figure sitting there for months. I keep thinking I’ll go back to it, because I used to get weirdly excited about adjusting the settings and fixing tiny defects, like… sanding one little shoulder for twenty minutes. But now I just look at it and it feels like another unfinished job.

And yoga, or walking in the woods—those used to feel good, like a reset. Now it’s more like, “I should do that,” the way I should take out the trash or answer an email. It doesn’t really pull me toward it. It’s just another task.

I mean, I used to be pretty good at keeping things moving—well, except when the software freezes, which happens all the time and everyone jokes about it—but this feels different. It’s like I’m the one freezing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if sad is the right word. I mean, maybe? It’s more like everything feels kind of muted, like when a monitor is on but the brightness is turned way down. Sorry, that’s a weird comparison—I’ve been dealing with these awful screens at work, and one of them has this flicker that nobody else seems to notice.\n\nBut, yeah… down, I guess. I’m not sure how to measure it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. On edge like nervous? I don’t think of it that way exactly. I mean, I get tense when people are waiting on me, but that’s also kind of normal at work, right? There’s always some deadline or someone needing a drawing revised, and then the printer jams even though it’s supposedly “fixed”—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed being anxious, specifically. Like I should do it, but I don’t actually… want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I kind of already said it, but the miniature printer thing is probably the clearest example. I used to care about getting the tiny details right, like supports and sanding and painting, and now the same half-finished figure has just been sitting there for months.\n\nAnd yoga, or going for walks in the woods—those used to feel good, or at least like something I wanted to do. Now it’s more like, “Okay, I should do that,” like checking off a chore. It doesn’t really pull me in anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any changes in your appetite or sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been tracking it. Appetite, I mean, some days I just eat because it’s time, not because I’m hungry, but I don’t know if that’s different enough to count.\n\nSleep is… hard to say too. I’ll go to bed, and then I start thinking about whether I set up the print files correctly at work, or if I forgot to answer an email, and then somehow it’s like I’m reorganizing my whole desk in my head. But I don’t know if that’s a sleep pattern change exactly. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself much—my scale is kind of shoved under the bathroom shelf with, like, extra shampoo bottles and stuff. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, I guess? But I also wear the same loose hoodie most of the time, so that’s probably not a great measure.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a significant change, at least not one I can point to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving or reacting more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m trying to be slow, but in meetings people have started kind of jumping in and finishing my sentences because I’m taking too long to answer. Which is embarrassing, because I do know what I’m trying to say, it’s just like it has to come through molasses first.\n\nAnd even just walking from my desk to the test chamber feels weirdly heavy, like I’m moving through thick syrup or something. I keep thinking about the floor tiles there, actually, because one of them has this little chipped corner and I notice it every time now… but, yeah, I guess I do feel slower than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, I guess. Maybe more than I should be.\n\nIt’s mostly stupid little things from the lab, like if I mislabeled something for a minute or had to redo a setup, or if I think maybe I slowed someone down. I’ll replay it over and over, like while I’m brushing my teeth or trying to make coffee, and then suddenly it’s not just, “I made a mistake,” it’s… “I’m bad at this, I shouldn’t be trusted,” and then it turns into “maybe I’m just a bad person.”\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic. It’s not like one specific huge thing. It’s tiny things. But my brain keeps making them feel like proof that I deserve whatever happens because of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having trouble sitting still, or is it more that you're feeling slowed down physically?\n\nPatient: More slowed down, I think. I’m not really fidgety… I mean, I pick at my sleeve sometimes, but that’s more because there’s this loose thread on it and it catches on things, and then I notice it for like ten minutes.\n\nBut, yeah, mostly slowed. Like in meetings, people have started finishing my sentences because I take too long to answer. And walking from my desk to the test chamber feels… heavy, like I’m moving through thick syrup.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… don’t think so? I haven’t really been keeping track.\n\nMy scale is under this basket of laundry in the bathroom, and I keep meaning to move it, but then I notice the basket has all these mismatched socks in it and I start sorting them, and then I’m late for work, so… I don’t actually know. My clothes don’t feel hugely different, I guess. So, no, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. Not like I have some big plan written out or anything, but… on my commute, I’ll catch myself thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t arrive anywhere.\n\nAnd then sometimes, when I’m driving, there’s this thought about steering off the road, and it scares me. Like, actually scares me enough that I grip the wheel really hard and pull over for a bit. I’ll sit there with the hazard lights on, and then I start worrying someone’s going to think my car broke down, or that I’m blocking something, and it becomes this whole… anyway.\n\nSo, yes. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself restless or having an urge to move around when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Not really an urge to move, no. And even walking from my desk to the test chamber feels like I’m moving through thick syrup. I don’t know why I said syrup—there’s this one hallway at work where the floor is always kind of sticky near the vending machines—but, yeah. Not constant motion. More the opposite.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an inner restlessness that makes you want to move around?\n\nPatient: No… not inner restlessness, exactly.
